Mosca's inequality (x + y > z): x = 10, y = 5, z = 8 → migration would finish too late under this scenario. z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.

Post-quantum maturity: Level 0 of 4 — no post-quantum cryptography. Next step: Inventory where classical public-key cryptography protects long-lived data and keys, and choose the first migration: usually a hybrid TLS key exchange and wrapped keys.

Methodology

The audit was performed by the QSI engine 1.3.0. Each analyzer emits facts (rule, parameters, evidence); finding texts are generated from the rule catalog. Methodology sources are listed under "Sources and normative documents".

Prioritization uses an explainable weighted model (severity, exploitability, exposure, confidentiality/integrity/availability impact weighted by the "Crypto exchange, custodian or wallet" profile, asset criticality, urgency, ease of remediation) with a confidence multiplier. CVSS v3.1 is applied only to technical vulnerabilities with a defined vector. Long-term quantum risks use Mosca's inequality; their severity is capped at Medium and they are never presented as exploitable vulnerabilities.

Finding classes: confirmed technical vulnerability; known vulnerable component (a dependency version named in an advisory; whether the affected code is reachable is assessed separately, and a component confirmed by review becomes a vulnerability); confirmed configuration error; potential cryptographic risk; long-term post-quantum risk; improvement recommendation; insufficient data. Heuristic findings are marked preliminary; analyst decisions are shown next to the findings.

QSI-2026-19546 · CODE-QV-ASYMMETRICMedium

Quantum-vulnerable ECDH: key wrapping and data encryption at rest (1 file(s))

Long-term post-quantum riskConfidence: Mediumpreliminary — requires further verification

3 reference(s) to ECDH across 1 file(s), e.g. ethereum/p2p/src/main/java/org/hyperledger/besu/ethereum/p2p/rlpx/handshake/ecies/ECIESHandshaker.java:174. Role, judged from paths, function names and nearby code: key wrapping and data encryption at rest. Keys or data encrypted to these public keys are stored: anyone who copies them now decrypts them once a CRQC exists (harvest now, decrypt later).

Affected asset
source-code:ECDH:key_wrapping
Data source

Detection method

CVSS v3.1
not applicable
Mosca's inequality (x + y > z)
x=10.0, y=5.0, z=8.0 → migration would finish too late under this scenario

Potential impact

Long-term risk depends on what the algorithm protects: signatures (authenticity until migration) or key exchange/encryption (retroactive decryption of recorded data).

Limitations of the check

This is a forward-looking risk that depends on the arrival of a cryptographically relevant quantum computer (CRQC). No such computer is publicly known to exist today; the timeline is uncertain. It is not a present-day exploitable vulnerability. Heuristic pattern match on source text without data-flow analysis. The usage context (security-relevant or not) must be confirmed manually; treat as preliminary.

Recommendation

Wrap keys with ML-KEM or a hybrid KEM (X25519 + ML-KEM-768) and re-wrap the stored keys; data that may already have been copied needs its own assessment.

Retest procedure

Re-run the inventory after refactoring.

Assumed years to CRQC z8 (early)8

Planning assumptions: x for key establishment, x for signatures, y are the defaults of the risk profile “Crypto exchange, custodian or wallet”, not the client's figures; with the client's actual lifetimes the verdict can change.

Post-quantum maturity: Level 0 of 4 — no post-quantum cryptography

  • Classical only — key wrapping and data encryption at rest: ECDH (1 file(s))

Next step: Inventory where classical public-key cryptography protects long-lived data and keys, and choose the first migration: usually a hybrid TLS key exchange and wrapped keys.

Levels: 0 none; 1 present, not wired; 2 in use outside the critical paths; 3 hybrid in a critical path; 4 post-quantum by default in the critical paths (root of trust, key wrapping, channel key exchange). Judged from code and manifests.

What the quantum-vulnerable algorithms do

RoleAlgorithms (files)IDFirst step
key wrapping and data encryption at restECDH (1)QSI-2026-19546Wrap keys with ML-KEM or a hybrid KEM (X25519 + ML-KEM-768) and re-wrap the stored keys; data that may already have been copied needs its own assessment
transaction and message signingBLS12-381 (11), ECDSA (6), RSA (1)QSI-2026-19543, QSI-2026-19544, QSI-2026-19545Follow each chain's post-quantum roadmap, avoid public-key reuse, and keep signers ready to add post-quantum signature schemes as chains adopt them
role not determinedECDSA (11), ECDH (4), BN254 (alt_bn128) (1)QSI-2026-19540, QSI-2026-19541, QSI-2026-19542Classify each usage, record data lifetimes, and plan migration to ML-DSA (FIPS 204) or SLH-DSA (FIPS 205), in a hybrid/composite form where the ecosystem supports it; introduce an abstraction layer for crypto-agility

Roles are judged from file paths, function names and the code around each match; review confirms them. Root-of-trust keys and wrapped keys come first: they are hard to replace or can be recorded today.

z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.

Quantum algorithms relevant to this inventory

Quantum algorithmEffectInventoried algorithms (rows)What it means for this audit
Shor's algorithm — factoring
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		breaks RSA (2)Breaks RSA at every key length: a longer modulus only raises the cost polynomially. Plan migration to ML-KEM (or a hybrid) for key establishment and ML-DSA / SLH-DSA for signatures; start with keys protecting data that must stay confidential for years (harvest now, decrypt later) and with long-lived signatures.
Shor's algorithm — discrete logarithms in finite fields
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		breaks BLS12-381 (11), BN254 (alt_bn128) (1)Breaks finite-field Diffie–Hellman and DSA at every size. Move key establishment to ML-KEM (hybrid with X25519 or ECDH during the transition) and signatures to ML-DSA or SLH-DSA.
Shor's algorithm — elliptic-curve discrete logarithms
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		breaks BLS12-381 (11), BN254 (alt_bn128) (1), ECDH (5), ECDSA (23)Elliptic-curve keys are expected to fall before RSA keys of comparable classical strength. On blockchains, coins whose public key is already on chain (reused addresses, pay-to-public-key and Taproot outputs) are exposed first; plan the move to post-quantum signatures and avoid key reuse in the meantime.
Grover's algorithm — key search and preimages
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		weakens (larger keys restore the margin) HMAC (1), Keccak-256 (29), SHA-2 (3), bcrypt (1)Not a break. 256-bit keys (AES-256, ChaCha20) keep a comfortable margin; AES-128 meets NIST category 1, while some authorities ask for 256-bit keys in quantum-resistant contexts (see the AES-128 entry). Prefer 256-bit keys for long-lived data; 256-bit hashes need no change for preimage resistance.
Brassard–Høyer–Tapp — collision search
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		weakens (larger keys restore the margin) Keccak-256 (29), SHA-2 (3)256-bit hashes (SHA-256, SHA3-256) remain adequate for collision resistance; 160-bit hashes are already weak classically (SHA-1 collisions are practical). Use 384/512-bit hashes where NIST categories 4–5 are required.

Effects: breaks — replace the algorithm; weakens — larger keys or outputs restore the margin; included in standard parameters — no action at standard parameter sets; only with superposition queries — not applicable to ordinary deployments.

Limitations and assumptions

  • This report presents automated and documented analysis results for the stated scope. It is not a legal opinion, a certification or an attestation of compliance with any standard.
  • z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.
  • keys — error (not checked): ethereum/api/src/main/java/org/hyperledger/besu/ethereum/api/jsonrpc/authentication/JWTAuthOptionsFactory.java line 118: public key could not be parsed (ValueError)

QSI-2026-19546 · P4 · 90 daysroot-cause fix

Plan migration of ECDH to Hybrid key establishment with ML-KEM (FIPS 203), e.g. X25519MLKEM768 in TLS 1.3 or mlkem768x25519-sha256 in SSH (or a hybrid scheme) once supported by your libraries, protocols and counterparties.

Rationale
Removes dependence on a quantum-vulnerable public-key primitive.
Expected outcome
The asset uses a NIST-standardized post-quantum or hybrid algorithm.
Complexity
high
Dependencies
Library/protocol support (e.g. OpenSSL ≥ 3.5, HSM firmware), interoperability with peers.
Side effects
Larger keys/signatures, possible performance and compatibility impact.
Tests before change
Interoperability tests in a staging environment.
Tests after change
Verify the negotiated/used algorithm on the production asset.
Closure criteria
Re-audit shows Hybrid key establishment with ML-KEM (FIPS 203), e.g. X25519MLKEM768 in TLS 1.3 or mlkem768x25519-sha256 in SSH or a hybrid scheme in use.

Sources and normative documents

Reviewed: 2026-10-13

  1. A. Chailloux, M. Naya-Plasencia, A. Schrottenloher — An Efficient Quantum Collision Search Algorithm and Implications on Symmetric Cryptography (ASIACRYPT 2017; IACR ePrint 2017/847) — IACR ePrint. https://eprint.iacr.org/2017/847 (peer-reviewed article: Õ(2^{2n/5}) time, O(n) qubits, Õ(2^{n/5}) classical memory)
  2. C. Chevignard, P.-A. Fouque, A. Schrottenloher — Reducing the Number of Qubits in Quantum Discrete Logarithms on Elliptic Curves (IACR ePrint 2026/280) — IACR ePrint. https://eprint.iacr.org/2026/280 (revision of a EUROCRYPT 2026 paper: 3.12n + o(n) qubits; for n = 256, 1193 qubits and 22 runs of 2^38.98 Toffoli gates)
  3. C. Gidney — How to factor 2048 bit RSA integers with less than a million noisy qubits (arXiv:2505.15917) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15917 (preprint (2025-05-21): < 1 million noisy qubits, < 1 week, 0.1% gate error, 1 µs surface-code cycle)
  4. C. Gidney, M. Ekerå — How to factor 2048 bit RSA integers in 8 hours using 20 million noisy qubits (Quantum 5, 433) — Quantum. https://doi.org/10.22331/q-2021-04-15-433 (peer-reviewed article (2021): 10^-3 gate error, 1 µs cycle, 10 µs reaction time)
  5. C. Zalka — Grover's quantum searching algorithm is optimal (Phys. Rev. A 60, 2746, 1999; arXiv:quant-ph/9711070) — APS. https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevA.60.2746 (peer-reviewed article: optimal for any success probability; parallel search gains no more than splitting the space)
  6. Common Vulnerability Scoring System v3.1: Specification Document — FIRST. https://www.first.org/cvss/v3.1/specification-document (final)
  7. D. J. Bernstein — Cost analysis of hash collisions: Will quantum computers make SHARCS obsolete? (2009) — cr.yp.to. https://cr.yp.to/hash/collisioncost-20090823.pdf (paper dated 2009-08-23)
  8. D. Litinski — How to compute a 256-bit elliptic curve private key with only 50 million Toffoli gates (arXiv:2306.08585) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.08585 (preprint (2023-06-14): 6000 modules × 1152 physical qubits, one key per 10 minutes under its architecture assumptions)
  9. FIPS 203 — Module-Lattice-Based Key-Encapsulation Mechanism Standard (ML-KEM) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/203/final (final (August 2024))
  10. FIPS 204 — Module-Lattice-Based Digital Signature Standard (ML-DSA) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/204/final (final (August 2024))
  11. FIPS 205 — Stateless Hash-Based Digital Signature Standard (SLH-DSA) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/205/final (final (August 2024))
  12. G. Brassard, P. Høyer, A. Tapp — Quantum Algorithm for the Collision Problem (LATIN '98; arXiv:quant-ph/9705002) — Springer / arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/quant-ph/9705002 (peer-reviewed article: collisions of r-to-one functions in O((N/r)^{1/3}) expected evaluations)
  13. J. Proos, C. Zalka — Shor's discrete logarithm quantum algorithm for elliptic curves (Quantum Inf. Comput. 3(4), 2003; arXiv:quant-ph/0301141) — Quantum Information & Computation. https://doi.org/10.26421/QIC3.4-3 (peer-reviewed article)
  14. L. K. Grover — A fast quantum mechanical algorithm for database search (STOC 1996) — ACM. https://doi.org/10.1145/237814.237866 (peer-reviewed article)
  15. M. Amy et al. — Estimating the cost of generic quantum pre-image attacks on SHA-2 and SHA-3 (SAC 2016; arXiv:1603.09383) — Springer / arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/1603.09383 (peer-reviewed article: SHA-256 about 2^153.8 surface-code cycles on 2^12.6 logical qubits; SHA3-256 about 2^146.5 cycles on 2^20)
  16. M. Cain et al. — Shor's algorithm is possible with as few as 10,000 reconfigurable atomic qubits (arXiv:2603.28627) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28627 (preprint (2026-03-30): P-256 in a few days with 26,000 physical neutral-atom qubits; RSA-2048 one to two orders of magnitude longer)
  17. M. Ekerå, J. Gärtner — A high-level comparison of state-of-the-art quantum algorithms for breaking asymmetric cryptography (arXiv:2405.14381) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14381 (preprint (2024-05-23): Regev-style algorithms show no overall advantage yet for cryptographic RSA and finite-field sizes)
  18. M. Mosca — Cybersecurity in an Era with Quantum Computers: Will We Be Ready? (IEEE Security & Privacy, 2018) — IEEE. https://doi.org/10.1109/MSP.2018.3761723 (peer-reviewed article; source of the x + y > z migration inequality)
  19. M. Roetteler, M. Naehrig, K. Svore, K. Lauter — Quantum resource estimates for computing elliptic curve discrete logarithms (ASIACRYPT 2017) — IACR / arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.06752 (peer-reviewed article: at most 9n + 2⌈log2 n⌉ + 10 qubits and 448 n^3 log2 n + 4090 n^3 Toffoli gates)
  20. NIST IR 8547 — Transition to Post-Quantum Cryptography Standards — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/ir/8547/final (final (2026-10-27; the initial public draft of November 2024 is obsoleted). Tables 2 and 4: ECDSA, EdDSA and RSA signatures, finite-field and elliptic-curve DH and MQV, and RSA key establishment are deprecated after 2030 and disallowed after 2035 at every key size — the draft deprecated only the 112-bit level. A composite (hybrid) key establishment stays FIPS 140-validatable while one input comes from an approved scheme. Repeats the federal deadlines of OMB M-26-15: post-quantum key establishment by 2030-12-31 and signatures by 2031-12-31 for high value assets and high impact systems)
  21. NIST — Post-Quantum Cryptography Standardization: Evaluation Criteria, Security (Call for Proposals, Section 4.A.5) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/Projects/Post-Quantum-Cryptography/Post-Quantum-Cryptography-Standardization/Evaluation-Criteria/Security-(Evaluation-Criteria) (security categories: AES-128 key search 2^170/MAXDEPTH quantum gates, AES-192 2^233/MAXDEPTH, AES-256 2^298/MAXDEPTH; MAXDEPTH 2^40–2^96)
  22. P. W. Shor — Polynomial-Time Algorithms for Prime Factorization and Discrete Logarithms on a Quantum Computer (SIAM J. Comput., 1997) — SIAM. https://doi.org/10.1137/S0097539795293172 (peer-reviewed article)
  23. R. Babbush et al. — Securing Elliptic Curve Cryptocurrencies against Quantum Vulnerabilities: Resource Estimates and Mitigations (arXiv:2603.28846) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28846 (preprint (2026-03-30, v2 2026-04-15): 256-bit ECDLP with < 1200 logical qubits and < 90 M Toffoli gates (or < 1450 and < 70 M); < 500,000 physical qubits at 10^-3 error, minutes)
  24. S. Jaques, M. Naehrig, M. Roetteler, F. Virdia — Implementing Grover oracles for quantum key search on AES and LowMC (EUROCRYPT 2020; arXiv:1910.01700) — Springer / arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/1910.01700 (peer-reviewed article: lower cost estimates for the AES key searches that define the NIST categories)
